- **R61 — Mockup-informed theatrical design direction, co-created with the owner (M6 artwork, D17; 2026-09-16).** Supersedes/refines R60's initial grindhouse palette after the owner supplied `MOCKUP.png`, `FONTWORK1/2.png`, `MOCKUP-HTML.html` + a ChatGPT brief in `/home/minnix/design-elements/` (reference-only — NOT to be copied; major decisions co-made with the owner). Confirmed: **(1) Fonts** self-hosted SIL OFL WOFF2 under `static/fonts/` (R6, no CDN): Monoton = logo only; Bebas Neue = film titles + section headings; Oswald (variable 200–700) = nav/metadata/buttons/small headings; Archivo (variable 400–800) = body/reviews; each font's `OFL.txt` alongside. **(2) Palette** layered near-black browns (`#090807`/`#11100e`/`#1b1713` + panel tones), dirty cream `#e9dfcb`, red as theatrical lighting (not a generic link color), thin amber/brown borders (`#5a432c`). **(3) Texture** restrained; no grunge on content cards; the feed stays clean. **(4) Layout** header = logo-left / search-middle / profile-right + a nav row (Home, My Films, Find People, Import, Export — real pages only; "Reviews" deferred until a `/reviews/` index page exists; owner-confirmed 2026-09-16; NO corner script); NO hero/featured-film band for now; home = 2-pane with a larger left pane = "Now Playing" feed (not "in the Feed") and a right sidebar = a "Trending Films" ticket stub (the ~5–10 instance films with the most reviews in the last 30 days, counting written or star-only ratings — owner-confirmed 2026-09-16) + "Popular Genres" pills (local aggregate of `Film.genres` — no runtime TMDB calls); NO "Tonight's Recommendations". Sub-increments A (done, `411dad8`), B (header), C (home 2-pane + sidebar). Owner may veto any of it; the design-elements folder is reference only.
- **R62 — Marquee header shape (M6 artwork step B; owner decisions 2026-09-16).** Four open points on the R61 header restructure, confirmed with the owner before building: **(1) No tagline** under the neon wordmark — the mockup's small letter-spaced line below REELTALK does not carry over. **(2) Wide marquee band** — the header spans the viewport as a full-width dark strip and its inner container widens to 72rem, while page content stays in the existing 40rem column until sub-increment C widens the home layout; search is truly page-centered via a `1fr minmax(0, 34rem) 1fr` grid (logo left / search middle / profile right; authed handle links to the user's profile page). **(3) Centered nav row** under the marquee (not left-aligned); active page = cream text + red underline + faint neon glow; "Reviews" stays deferred until a `/reviews/` index exists. **(4) Anonymous nav is Home-only** — My Films / Find People / Import / Export are login-gated views, so they render only for authenticated users (no dead-end links); anonymous account actions stay in the top row (Log in / Sign up). Owner may veto.
- **R63 — Site-wide grunge texture + steel dividers (M6 artwork; owner brief 2026-09-16).** Supersedes the R60/R61 "no full-page grunge / header-band texture" stance by explicit owner instruction: the whole page sits on a **real `body` background** layering `rgba(9,8,7,.86)` scrim over the owner-supplied grunge tile (`reeltalk/social/static/images/grunge-background.png`, repeat at 1000px) so the texture reads at ~14%; content panels (`.feed-item`, `.review`) stay nearly opaque `rgba(14,12,10,.88)` and body copy clean. The header's own marquee texture is **removed** — it carries the same surface as the page. Both page rules — under the header, above the footer — use the owner-picked light steel `--divider: #c1c1be` at 3px, and sit *under* the grunge via a tile strip blended over each line with `mix-blend-mode: soft-light` (painting scrim/tile over them instead buries the lines; the tile is opaque so blending is required). Mobile step-downs ride along: wordmark 2.2rem + collapsed gap below 40rem, wrapping `.review-head`, breakable remote handles. New static asset raises the suite baseline to **724 passed + 5 skipped**. Owner reviewed live and approved before commit (`9dc036b`).
- **R64 — Home 2-pane, rail rules, and the genre subfeed (M6 artwork step C; owner decisions 2026-09-17).** Eight open points were put to the owner before building; all landed on the recommended option. **(1) Width** — the home container widens to **72rem**, flush with the marquee band (feed's left edge under the wordmark, rail's right edge under the profile corner), via a `main_class` block hook in `base.html`; every other page keeps the 40rem reading column. Rail 22rem, stacking below 64rem. **(2) Home top** — the feed **leads**: no duplicated site name, description, or "Signed in as" line for authenticated users (the header wordmark carries the brand). **(3) Trending window** — **strict 30 days, no top-up**: a thin month shows a short list rather than padding with older reviews or stretching the window (weighed against all-time top-up and auto-widening). **(4) Stub length** — 8 films (R61's 5–10 range). **(5) Genre scope** — genres of **reviewed films only**, not shelves and not the whole catalog: the imported archive puts 1383/1384 films on a shelf, so any shelf-inclusive tally just echoes the TMDB import and would light pills that open empty pages. **(6) Pills are a browse surface, not search links** — each opens a new public **`/genre/<slug>/` subfeed** of member reviews for that genre (owner's explicit ask), newest-first, paginated 20/page, showing every review the instance holds **including federated mirrors**; slugs resolve only for genres carrying live reviews, so an unknown or stale slug 404s and no pill dead-ends. **(7) Anonymous home** — same 2-pane: the sign-up CTA occupies the feed slot and the rail stays public (film pages and the genre subfeed are already login-open). **(8) Rail artwork** — each card is titled by an **owner-supplied ticket-stub graphic** above it, so the cards themselves have no visible heading (an `.visually-hidden` `<h2>` preserves it for assistive tech); a CSS-drawn paper stub was built first and replaced by the owner's own artwork in review. Assets are owner-supplied originals (REWRITE.md rule 4), optimized on commit to ~114 KB each with transparency kept, their root-folder source copies deleted with owner approval. **Flagged for veto, accepted as built:** rail counts are **instance-wide** — a blocked user's review still feeds a film's trending tally and a genre count (viewer-relative aggregates would recompute per request), while the subfeed list itself honors R55/R56 blocks. Owner may veto any of it.
- **R65 — Header/footer dividers become painted worn-chrome rails (M6 artwork polish; owner brief 2026-09-18).** Supersedes R63's divider technique by explicit owner instruction ("make this effect more dramatic so that the lines really look like grungy chrome", `MOCKUP.png` cited). Decided: **(1) The rail is art, not CSS.** An original strip generated for this project (`reeltalk/social/static/images/chrome-rail.png`, seamless 2400×17, 49 KB) is painted `repeat-x` on `.site-header::after` / `.site-footer::before`; REWRITE.md rule 4 is met because the project authored it — no external or reference-folder asset. Rejected alternatives, both tried live and abandoned: blending the grunge tile over a flat rule (the tile is dark, so `hard-light`/`overlay`/`multiply` crush the metal to black and `soft-light` — the R63 state — is invisible against the near-black page), and pure-gradient "corrosion" (`repeating-linear-gradient` reads as a dashed hairline). **(2) The base under the rail goes dark** — the 3px header/footer borders switch from light steel to `--black-2`, because a light border shows through the strip's corrosion pits and turns the gaps gray; consequently the **`--divider` token is removed** (the light steel now lives in the art). **(3) The footer is phase-shifted** (`background-position: -1150px 0`) so the two rails don't display an identical pattern. **(4) Drama level** — of four generated variants the owner's brief pointed at the most worn (`d-max`), with rust warmth, core thickness (4px shipped) and a warm bloom kept as live dials; re-tuning is a regeneration + asset swap, no CSS change. Owner reviewed the result live at :3030 and approved before commit.
- **R66 — Site-wide background becomes a weathered plate; prominence comes from contrast, not brightness (M6 artwork polish; owner brief 2026-09-18).** Refines R63's texture pass by explicit owner instruction (the distressed background was too subtle to notice next to `MOCKUP.png`; target = "more worn and weathered"). Decided: **(1) Measure first.** Background-only sampling gave our page **lum 9.1 / stddev 1.9** against the mockup's **12–22 / 15–32**, and showed the owner's tile itself was fine (14.5 stddev) — the `.86` scrim was the culprit. **(2) Do not just thin the scrim.** Simulated CSS said hitting the reference stddev with the raw tile costs mean luminance 43–56 (muddy grey): the reference is **bimodal** — near-black areas plus sparse bright wear — so prominence must be bought with contrast at a low mean. **(3) Ship a derived plate** (`grunge-weathered.png`): crush the tile's midtones so its average stays near-black, carve deep large-scale worn blotches downward, then screen on dense dust flecks / short scuffs / long streaky scratches — all periodic or wrapped so it tiles seamlessly. Derived from the owner's supplied asset, so grain identity and REWRITE.md rule 4 both hold; quantized to **228 KB**. **(4) Scrim `.86 → .62` as a pure prominence dial:** measured live, page-background stddev **1.9 → 8.0** with mean luminance *falling* 9.1 → 7.5 — more visible wear without washing out the page or touching text contrast (content panels and body copy unchanged). **(5) Keep the original tile** in the repo as source even though nothing references it now — deletion was offered and not requested. Rejected after being built and reviewed: uniformly lifting plate brightness (raises mean, buys nothing), pale worn patches above ~lum 50 (read as clouds/bokeh dots), clustered saturated warm speckle (reads as colour noise). Dials left live: scrim `.72` / `.62` (shipped) / `.52`, plus dust and scratch density in the generator. Owner reviewed live at :3030 and approved ("That looks great") before commit; **owner explicitly asked for this increment to be pushed**.
- **R67 — The nav page indicator becomes a lit neon tube (M6 artwork polish; owner brief 2026-09-18).** Owner asked for `MOCKUP.png`'s glowing red nav indicator (reference-only, as ever). Decided: **(1) Measure the reference before writing CSS** — probing the mockup's tube at its core (`MOCKUP.png` y≈99–104) shows it is **not** a red line but a 2–3px tube with a **white-hot core** (peak `255,226,225`) inside saturated red `(245,31,35)`, bloom reaching ~8px above / ~10px below before merging into the backdrop; its active label also carries a warm bloom hugging the glyphs (R61 already had a faint `text-shadow` version — kept unchanged). **(2) A border cannot express that** — the marker moves from `.site-nav a.active`'s `border-bottom-color` to a positioned `.site-nav a.active::after` bar (3px, `bottom: -3px`) with a gradient cross-section (`--red` upper glass edge → white filament row → `--red-bright` lower body) plus four shadow stops of spill (3 / 9 / 20 / 40px). The transparent 2px border stays on the anchor so nav metrics, wrapping and the mobile header are untouched. **(3) Four recipes were rendered side by side over the real stylesheet + backdrop** (`.qwen/tmp/neon-lab.html` → headless-Chromium → annotated sheet) rather than guessed at: A mockup-faithful tight bloom, B the wordmark's existing 4-stop glow family, C dramatic tube, D flat line + short bloom. The owner picked **C**, and scoped it to **the header nav row only** — deliberately *not* applied to the `.tabs a.active` underline on the films page and no hover warm-up, so this stays one motif in one place rather than a site-wide indicator system. **(4) No new asset, no new token.** Pure CSS, so unlike R65/R66 there is nothing to quantize and the suite total does not move (no file added ⇒ no new clean-room guard case). Dials left live: spill radius (40px → 28/55), how white the filament row runs, and dropping the tube onto the chrome rail (~10px lower) so it rests on the metal the way the mockup's does. Owner reviewed live at :3030 and approved ("Looks good") before commit.
- **R68 — The wordmark is relit as red glass with a white-hot filament; strokes must scale in em (M6 artwork polish; owner brief 2026-09-18).** Follow-on from R67: with the nav tube lit, the owner found the ReelTalk wordmark "somewhat dim compared to it". Decided: **(1) Measure the gap** — our `.brand` was a flat `#f9a58f` fill whose rendered luminance **hard-capped at 193 with zero pixels above 200**: no hot spots anywhere, so it read as paint. The mockup's wordmark is **bimodal** (max 250, p90 220, 22.8% of lit pixels ≥200) — deep red glass plus white-hot filament highlights, the same contrast-at-low-mean lesson as R66. **(2) Fill = filament, stroke = glass.** `color: #fff4ee` with `-webkit-text-stroke` in `--red` painted **over** the fill (default paint order), which carves red back into each Monoton bar and leaves a narrow white-hot core — the only recipe of seven tested that reproduces the reference's per-stroke structure. Bloom rebuilt from four stops to six (`2px #fff → 5/12px --red-bright → 26px --red → 48px → 80px`). Rejected after rendering: plain hot fill (goes white/pink, 25% of pixels near-white), gradient-clipped tube (no per-stroke core), heavier 1.6px stroke (swallows the filament — flat red, max 211), warm step-up (brighter salmon, still no true core). **(3) The stroke is `0.0174em`, never px** — under 40rem the brand steps down to 2.2rem where Monoton's bars are ~2–3px wide, and a fixed **1px edge ate the filament entirely: mobile measured max lum 171, nothing above 200 — darker than the flat fill it replaced**. Caught by screenshotting at 390px; em-scaling fixes it with no media-query special case (mobile now max 223, 7.5% ≥200). **(4) Scope**: `.brand` only; the R67 nav tube, search trim and header layout are untouched. Live result: wordmark **max 245 / 9.8% of lit pixels ≥200** against the reference's 250 / 22.8%, still red-dominant rather than washed out. Dials left live: stroke weight (0.012em redder / 0.023em whiter) and outer spill reach. Owner reviewed live at :3030 and approved ("That looks great"), asking for this one to be **committed and pushed**.
- **R69 — The header's own text takes the film-title face (M6 artwork polish; owner decision 2026-09-19).** Owner raised it: the nav bar's font bothered them and should become the face the Trending Films titles use. Diagnosis from the stylesheet alone — `.site-nav a` named `var(--ui)` (Oswald, condensed) while `.trending-film` names no family and inherits `var(--body)` (Archivo) at 600 — so this is Oswald → Archivo on the header's text. **The face swap is a width decision, not a taste one:** measuring the six signed-in nav items gives 247px (Oswald) → 311px (+26%) for a straight Archivo-600 match, and the nav row already wraps at 390px, so what differs between recipes is *where the line breaks* — the exact-match recipe pushes `Import` onto line 2, the stepped-down one does not. Owner chose **Archivo 600 at .88rem with .02em tracking** (285px, today's break preserved) and extended scope to **the top-right user links**, so nav row + Log out + profile link now read Archivo 600 while metadata, buttons, tabs and genre pills keep Oswald. Structure matters here: the two selectors were **removed from the `--ui` role list** rather than overridden in place (a second opinion on the same property is how type systems rot), Log out is named explicitly because the `button` role otherwise claims it, and the profile link steps down with the nav to keep a size parity it already had. Two durable lessons: **headless Chromium cannot render a phone width directly** — `--window-size` clamps at ~500px and `--force-device-scale-factor` doesn't shrink the layout viewport in `headless=new`, so a true 390 CSS-px render needs a same-origin 390px iframe (media queries follow it; `--allow-file-access-from-files` lets the parent read it back), and any earlier "measured at 390" claim predating this note was really 500px; and **verify against the deployed container, not the working tree** — this item's first green report was premature because the image had not been rebuilt, so the live page was still serving Oswald while the file on disk said Archivo.
- **R70 — People get faces: one avatar ring on the base `.avatar`, sized by surface (M6 artwork polish; owner decisions 2026-09-19).** Owner asked for `MOCKUP.png`'s avatar in the marquee corner and beside each feed author. Decided: **(1) The disc is another light fixture, not a photo frame.** Measuring the reference gave a ~2px warm-cream ring `(217,195,179)` with saturated red hugging the portrait *inside* it — R67/R68's hot-core-in-coloured-glass motif — so of four rendered recipes the owner took **A** (`border: 2px solid var(--avatar-ring)` + `inset 0 0 0 2px rgba(196,30,24,.85)` + a faint 7px spill) over B neon tube (loudest; competes with the nav indicator), C worn chrome bezel (rhymes with R65 but measured lowest-contrast on the weathered plate) and D plain hairline (the disc edge disappears). **(2) The ring belongs on base `.avatar`, not per surface.** The profile pages already had a 3rem disc with a tan `1px var(--border)` hairline; that rule is **deleted** and the recipe moved up to the base selector, so corner, profile, My Films and all row heads share one treatment — the owner's second note ("the profile ring looks tan") was answered by the same edit rather than an override. New token `--avatar-ring: #d9c3b3`; `flex: none` added so a long remote handle cannot squeeze a disc in a flex row. **(3) Size is one modifier, not three.** `.avatar-row` (2.05rem) is the only size rule; when the owner asked the corner to match the profile disc, the right change was **deleting** `.avatar-header` from CSS and template — the header inherits base 3rem — rather than leaving a rule that restates the default. Measured: a bigger corner avatar is *free*, because `.header-top` height is set by the wordmark row (110px @1440 / 127.7px @true-390, unchanged), and there is no horizontal overflow at either width. **(4) Structure over duplication:** the disc rides **inside** the existing `.user-name` profile link (`inline-flex`, `gap: .7rem` ≈ the reference's 12px) so the corner stays one tab stop instead of gaining a second link to the same page; row discs are decorative (`alt=""` / `aria-hidden`) and the three `.review-head` call sites repeat the `{% if …avatar %}` block in the template (matching `profile.html`) rather than gaining a partial for three uses. **(5) Scope: all three surfaces** that share `.review-head` — home feed, film-page review list, `/genre/<slug>/` — by owner decision, because splitting them means one component looks two ways. **(6) Placeholder is CSS, not an asset:** `.avatar-placeholder` became a hand-drawn silhouette plate (head + shoulders over red→black), which also restyles the profile page's empty disc; no file added ⇒ suite stays **751 passed + 5 skipped**. Dials left open: disc sizes and the .7rem gap, linking feed avatar+handle to the profile, the mockup's account **chevron** (implies a dropdown menu we do not have), and `user_films.html`'s missing `{% else %}` branch — My Films renders no disc at all for a user with no avatar. Owner reviewed live at :3030 and approved ("Looks good"), asking for the commit **and push**.
- **R71 — Red buttons become lit controls; the footer gains three zones and a neon sign-off (M6 artwork polish; owner brief 2026-09-19).** One brief, three asks, all aimed at `MOCKUP.png` (reference-only as ever): replace the striped red buttons with the reference's mottled-red / neon-outline **Read Reviews** button and carry that aesthetic onto the Getting Started step circles; model the footer on the mockup (all-caps Bebas wordmark + tagline, small Archivo links in the middle); emulate its right-hand red neon cursive **"Keep Watching."** with a film reel. Decided: **(1) The fill is art, not a gradient.** Sampling the reference gives interior luminance **mean 50.7 / p10 44 / p90 53** inside a ~2px rim peaking at `(248,134,128)` — mottling with the light in the edge — so an original seamless tile (`images/button-mottle.png`, 128×128, 5.1 KB) is painted over a dark red gradient; its marks deliberately run **both ways** (bright flecks lift, maroon pits eat) because one translucent overlay cannot mottle in a single direction. Rejected: pure CSS gradients for the wear (R65 already proved `repeating-linear-gradient` reads as a hairline pattern, and the old control's stripe overlay is exactly that mistake). Rim + bloom reuse the R67/R68 motif so buttons, nav tube and wordmark are one lighting rig. Live measurement lands at median 50 / p10 40. **(2) Labels take the marquee face.** Owner asked for all-caps Bebas Neue on the red buttons; because **Bebas ships a single weight**, `font-weight: 400` is stated explicitly — inheriting the old 600 synthesises a faux-bold — with `1.1rem` / `.05em` to hold presence on a condensed face, and `button, .btn` was **removed from the `--ui` role list** rather than overridden (R69's precedent). Ghost controls that share the element (`form.inline button`, `.search-row .watchlist-btn`) are named explicitly to keep Oswald 600 sentence case: they need family, weight, **leading**, tracking and casing resets plus `box-shadow: none`, since a shared-rule edit silently rewrites them — the first build rendered "Add to watchlist" in tracked caps and shrank every ghost box by ~3px. A face swap must not move layout the owner did not ask about, so leading is tuned (`1.4` lit / `1.55` ghost) to hold the original 44.8 / 32.3px boxes. **(3) The footer is three zones on the existing chrome rail**, band widened to **72rem** to line up with the marquee (wordmark + tagline left, wayfinding centred — Getting Started / About This Site / Code → GitHub in a new tab — sign-off right), stacking centred ≤40rem. **(4) The sign-off needs a real script face:** we had none, so **Kaushan Script** is self-hosted under SIL OFL (latin subset + `OFL.txt`, new `--script` token) rather than faking cursive with an existing face; the **film reel is inline SVG authored in the template**, which keeps the art original and adds no binary. Three new files ⇒ suite baseline **754 passed + 5 skipped** (one clean-room guard case each). Owner reviewed live at :3030 and approved ("Looks good"), asking for the commit **and push**. Dials left open: fill mottle drama, rim thickness, bloom radius, label size/tracking, reel size, and whether the footer's link separators stay pipes or go to mockup-style spacing.
- **R72 — The lit controls' bloom is pulled in to the reference's tight halo; the other three dials are explicitly kept (M6 artwork polish; owner decision 2026-09-20).** Instead of raising a complaint the owner picked an **open dial** off R71's list — "Lit control dials" — so all four dials (fill mottle drama, rim thickness, bloom radius, label size/tracking) were measured against `MOCKUP.png` and against our deployed render before anything changed, and three were deliberately left alone. **Measured:** the mockup's Read Reviews button sits at x 355→558 / y 500→543 (203×43px) with a ~2px rim peaking at `(245,120,118)` — which ours already matched at `(255,106,91)` — and a label cap of ~14px (0.33 of box) against our ~15px (0.33). The **bloom was the only real outlier**: the reference throws **2px up / 4px down** at backdrop+4 while ours threw **10 up / 19 down**, i.e. its luminance sits at flat backdrop until ~3px out and lifts only at the rim. **Decided: (1) Tighten the bloom only** — `button, .btn` outer stops `4/13/28 → 3/7/12` (@ .8/.45/.18), hover `6/18/40 → 4/9/15`, `.step-num` `4/12 → 3/8` (@ .8/.42); inset glows, the `--neon-rim` border, the radius, the mottle tile and the Bebas caps labels are untouched. Live result: the button's ramp reaches backdrop at **~6px** (was ~12px). **(2) Keep the mottle drama** even though ours measures **spread 19 / σ 7.0** against the reference's **spread 10 / σ 5.3** — the owner judged the busier fill reads better at our small control sizes, where the reference's quiet weave would wash out. **(3) Keep uppercase labels**, having established that the mockup's own label is **mixed case** — caps is our deliberate house style, not an unfinished match, so R71's "which is what the reference's button label reads as" is corrected by this note. **Durable lessons:** *(a)* **mottle samples must exclude the label band *and* its blended edges** — sampling the reference's side columns gives spread 34 purely from glyph anti-aliasing, which looks like dramatic texture and is nothing of the kind; the honest figure comes from label-free rows only. *(b)* **A bloom lab must isolate one candidate per render** — both a vertical stack and a horizontal row of candidates produced garbage decay ladders because the *neighbour's* glow sat inside the measurement window (a genuinely 4px candidate read 18px); only per-page isolation measures what it names. *(c)* **A session minted with only `SESSION_KEY` is anonymous.** Django resolves `request.user` from `SESSION_KEY` **plus `HASH_SESSION_KEY`** (with `BACKEND_SESSION_KEY` for the backend), so the first verification pass here silently checked nothing behind login — the avatar ring read `None`, the nav tube read `None`, and `/find/` and `/preferences/import/` returned **302** while the harness reported "page may lack the probed nodes". An anonymous page still renders lit buttons, so colour and bloom numbers survive the mistake while everything gated is unchecked: **assert the session is authenticated before trusting a signed-in probe.** Scope held to the lit recipe — no template, asset, token or README change (the README's "boxed by a hot rim with real bloom" carries no number and stays true). Gate unchanged at **754 passed + 5 skipped in 336.80s**; both throwaway sessions revoked (`SessionStore().exists` → `False`). Owner reviewed live at :3030 and approved ("Looks good"), asking for the commit **and push**. Still open from R71 and untouched here: fill mottle drama, rim thickness, label size/tracking, footer reel size, footer pipe separators.
- **R73 — The film page breaks out of the side-by-side layout on a phone: centred plate plus stacked label-over-value fields (M6 artwork polish; owner decision 2026-09-20).** The owner's complaint was a *symptom* — metadata "squeezed to the right" — so the block was measured at a true 390 before anything was designed, which located it as structural rather than cosmetic: `.film` never stopped being a `display: flex` row, so the poster + gap left `.film-meta` 178px and `dt { min-width: 6rem }` took 96 of them, leaving each value **74px** — narrower than one cast name, which is why the cast ran to 13 lines. **Decided: (1) Break at 40rem, not a new breakpoint** — the header (R62/R69) and footer (R71) already step down there, so the whole page turns over in one place rather than at three different widths. **(2) Poster + title become one centred plate.** With the column freed to 358px, a left-aligned 160px poster strands most of the row; centring makes the top read as a deliberate plate and the left-aligned list below it reads as content. **(3) Stack the label above the value rather than shrinking the label column.** A narrower `dt` would have fixed the wrapping (254px for the cast) but kept every value indented past the page edge — still "to the right", which is what was actually complained about. The `dt` drops `min-width` entirely and is restyled as a small uppercase tracked slate label (`0.72rem` / `.14em`, still `--muted`), which also gives the metadata block its own rhythm without a new face: it stays Oswald, the metadata role from R60. **(4) Scope to `.film .meta-list`, not `.meta-list`** — `templates/about.html` shares the class and its three short values read fine in two columns, so the shared rule was left alone and verified unchanged at 390. **Durable lesson: a "squeezed to the right" report is about the *column*, not the type.** Measuring the four nested widths (`.film` → `.film-meta` → `dt` → `dd`) named the culprit in one pass; the fix could equally have been "smaller label column", which would have passed a screenshot check at a glance while leaving the complaint half-alive. Mobile poster size (still 160×240 in a 358px column) is left open as its own dial. CSS-only, one file, +37; desktop verified unchanged at 1440 (`flex row`, `dt` 96px, cast 324px / 3 lines); `scrollWidth == clientWidth == 390` before and after. README "The look" gained a **Film page** bullet because the surface had no entry there. Gate unchanged at **758 passed + 5 skipped in 328.32s**. Owner reviewed live at :3030 and approved ("Looks good"), asking for the commit **and push**.
- **R74 — Deploy-readiness increment 1: only the operator's TLS terminator may tell us the scheme, and cookies follow the transport they actually rode (owner brief 2026-09-20, commits `abbec07` + `62a4d5d`).** The first increment of the real-domain deploy work. Four things were decided, all of them verified against the deployed container rather than reasoned out from the tree. **(1) `SECURE_TRUSTED_PROXIES` does not exist.** Checked on the running image: Django **6.1.1** has no such setting, and `SECURE_PROXY_SSL_HEADER` believes `X-Forwarded-Proto` from *any* client that sends it — the documented footgun, with no built-in remedy. Because D14 puts TLS termination outside the app, the app has to know which peer the terminator is, so the gate is ours: `reeltalk/proxy_trust.py`'s `TrustedProxySchemeMiddleware` runs **first in `MIDDLEWARE`** and deletes `HTTP_X_FORWARDED_PROTO` unless `REMOTE_ADDR` falls inside `TRUSTED_PROXIES` (CIDR-parsed, memoised on the setting value so `override_settings` still takes effect without a chain rebuild, and an invalid CIDR raises `ImproperlyConfigured` at startup rather than silently mis-trusting). `SECURE_PROXY_SSL_HEADER` is set **only when the list is non-empty**, so a deploy that forgets `TRUSTED_PROXIES` can never end up believing a forwarded scheme from a stranger. `HttpRequest.scheme` has **no setter** in 6.1.1, so the mechanism is stripping the header, not assigning the scheme; with it gone, `_get_scheme()` falls back to the real transport on both WSGI (`environ["wsgi.url_scheme"]`) and ASGI (`scope["scheme"]`). The old behaviour was reproduced live before the fix: one spoofed header turned the actor id into `https://192.168.1.138:3030/…` — a stranger rewriting the identity we publish. After: actor, inbox, outbox, sharedInbox and keyid all stay on the real scheme. **(2) Cookies followed the transport, not the config.** All three flags were at Django defaults, so the session id travelled in the clear; `SECURE_COOKIES` (default true) now drives both `SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE` and `CSRF_COOKIE_SECURE`, and `SESSION_COOKIE_SAMESITE = "Lax"` is pinned rather than inherited. That immediately broke LAN access — verified live: with `Secure` unconditional the browser never returns `csrftoken` over `http://`, the POST 403s, and `http://192.168.1.138:3030` cannot hold a session at all. The owner's answer was to keep LAN HTTP **permanently**, so `reeltalk/cookie_policy.py`'s `SchemeAwareCookieMiddleware` decides the flag per request: https → `Secure`, plain http → stripped, with `HttpOnly` and `SameSite=Lax` preserved both ways. It sits **immediately after the gate** because it keys off `request.is_secure()`, which is only trustworthy once the gate has run — the suite pins that adjacency (`cookies == gate + 1`) and proves the composition: a trusted terminator reporting https restores `Secure`, the same header from `203.0.113.9` does not. The rule can **raise** the flag as well as lower it (a real https request gets `Secure` even with the static session setting off). `COOKIES_FOLLOW_SCHEME=false` restores the strict unconditional posture, so reverting is an env flip, not a code change. Accepted trade, stated plainly: a session started on the LAN in cleartext has a cookie readable by anyone on that network path. **(3) Two cutover-relevant facts measured, not assumed.** *Docker does not mask the peer IP here* — `EnableUserlandProxy: true` raised the risk that every inbound connection would arrive as the `172.18.0.1` bridge gateway and `TRUSTED_PROXIES=192.168.1.141/32` would never match, silently stripping the forwarded scheme and publishing `http://` actor IDs over real HTTPS; holding a connection open and decoding the container's `/proc/net/tcp` showed `local=172.18.0.3:3030 remote=192.168.1.138:…`, i.e. the **real source address**, so the trust list will match NPM. *The no-`Host`-header port-append trap:* `_get_raw_host()` only rebuilds the host from `SERVER_NAME`/`SERVER_PORT` when no `Host` header is present, and in that branch appends the port whenever it isn't 443 on a secure request — which is why the test client produced `testserver:80`. Production is safe only because NPM passes the original `Host` (proven: the app's `DisallowedHost` 400 carries Django's own security headers, so the Host arrived intact). **Cutover must confirm the actor id comes back as `https://reeltalk.minnix.dev/user/minnix/` with no `:3030`.** **(4) Dead `BASE_URL` deleted** — `settings.py:29` had zero references anywhere outside this file's own prose. **Cutover check (read-only, done ahead of the cutover):** `reeltalk.minnix.dev` is an **explicit CNAME** → `minnix.dev` → **`97.98.21.119`**, a public IP; a random subdomain NXDOMAINs, so it is not a wildcard. Cloudflare is DNS-only (NS `colin`/`faye.ns.cloudflare.com`, cert is Let's Encrypt not Cloudflare-issued, so no edge proxy). NPM holds a valid cert `CN=*.minnix.dev`, issuer LE `YE2`, valid 2026-08-03 → **2026-11-01**. Port 80 `301 → https://reeltalk.minnix.dev/`; 443 open, HTTP/2, `server: openresty`; **3030 closed/filtered from the public IP** (LAN hairpin works, so that is a real result). The site will therefore be genuinely public with no proxy hiding the origin. **Owner relaxed the earlier binding constraint: binding `0.0.0.0` is acceptable**, so increment 3's loopback-bind worry is off the table and the LAN interface stays reachable. **Verification:** gate on the tree as committed — ruff check + `format --check` green, `makemigrations --check` clean (no model change), pytest **790 passed + 5 skipped in 334.10s** from a fresh `docker compose run --rm web` container: baseline 758 + 18 proxy-trust + 9 cookie-policy + 1 middleware-adjacency + 1 from splitting the old `test_actor_endpoint_follows_forwarded_proto` into trusted/untrusted cases + 3 clean-room guard cases for the new files. Read back off the **deployed** container: `TRUSTED_PROXIES=[]`, `SECURE_PROXY_SSL_HEADER=None`, `SECURE_COOKIES=True`, `COOKIES_FOLLOW_SCHEME=True`, `SESSION_COOKIE_SAMESITE='Lax'`, `MIDDLEWARE[0]=…TrustedProxySchemeMiddleware`, `hasattr(settings,'BASE_URL')` False. Live LAN proof on `:3030` with a throwaway account: `GET /login/` sets `csrftoken … SameSite=Lax` with **no** `Secure`, `POST /login/` returns **302 → /** with `sessionid … HttpOnly; SameSite=Lax` and no `Secure`, and the home page renders the signed-in handle. Throwaway user deleted and re-checked absent; cookie jars removed.
- **R75 — Deploy-readiness increment 2: one trusted scheme, so publication and signature verification can never disagree (owner brief 2026-09-20).** R74 built the gate but left two doors open beside it: `absolute_uri()` and `_target_uri()` each did `request.headers.get("X-Forwarded-Proto", "").split(",")[0].strip() or request.scheme` — a second, ungated path to the scheme that ignored `TRUSTED_PROXIES` entirely. **(1) Collapse both onto `request.scheme` rather than keep the header read "just in case".** After R74 that value already *is* the terminator's forwarded proto when it came from a trusted peer, and the real transport otherwise; the header read adds nothing correct and one thing wrong. **(2) The signature site was the load-bearing one, not the cosmetic one.** `absolute_uri` decides what we *publish*; `_target_uri` decides the URI we compare against what a remote actually signed. With the header read in place an unverified peer could move that URI at will — deliver over plain http, send `X-Forwarded-Proto: https`, and the base we rebuild is https — so a signature made over a different channel could validate on ours. Folding it onto the gated scheme leaves exactly one party able to move it: the terminator we named. **(3) The header is inert, not merely distrusted**, proved as its own case, because the tempting wrong "fix" is to let the header *raise* the scheme: an http-signed target over http transport still verifies with a stray `X-Forwarded-Proto: https` riding along, so the collapse costs no real delivery. **(4) The tests had to stop lying first.** `_signed_request` built every request over http regardless of the URL it had signed, which is exactly why the old test needed the header to pass; it now mirrors the URL's own transport (`secure=`), so "signed https" means "delivered over https". The old single case became three (https/https verifies with no header present at all; https-signed over http transport with the header claiming https does **not**; http/http verifies with the header present), and `test_proxy_trust.py` gained a section that holds the signature fixed and varies only the peer — the axis the gate is actually drawn on. **Durable lesson: a control added in one layer must be audited against every consumer beneath it.** R74's gate was correct on its own terms, but two functions below it still read the raw header, so the guarantee was partial — and a partial control in the signature path is worse than none, because it *looks* enforced. After adding a gate, grep for the header by name and read every hit; do not assume the gate covers the downstream. **Measured, not assumed:** live over real TCP in throwaway containers so the deployed stack's `TRUSTED_PROXIES=[]` was never loosened — trusted terminator + `Host: reeltalk.minnix.dev` → `https://reeltalk.minnix.dev/user/minnix/` and `https://reeltalk.minnix.dev/inbox/` with **no `:3030`** (R74's port-append trap stays shut while a real `Host` header is present); same container without the forwarded header → `http://…`; a second container trusting only `192.168.1.141/32` stayed `http://…` under the identical spoofed header from `.138`. Deployed `:3030` readback: no `headers.get("X-Forwarded-Proto"` anywhere under `reeltalk/activitypub/`. The only scheme consumer left outside the gate is `reeltalk/cookie_policy.py:34`, deliberately one slot after it and pinned there. **Gate:** `ruff check` / `format --check` (84 files) / `makemigrations --check` clean, pytest **794 passed + 5 skipped in 344.45s** — baseline 790 + 4 new cases.
- **R76 — Deploy-readiness increments 3 & 4: the plain-HTTP port's firewall expectation is documented rather than re-bound, and every signup CTA follows the instance policy (owner brief 2026-09-20).** Increment 3 was expected to change the port binding and did not: `docker-compose.yml` publishes `${WEB_PORT:-3030}:3030` with no bind address (`0.0.0.0`), the owner has explicitly accepted that, and R74 already measured 3030 closed/filtered from the public IP, so the only honest work left was to write down **why** the binding is safe and where it isn't. The `.env.example` note carries the distinction that matters most: **R74's forwarded-scheme gate is not an access control.** It decides whom to *believe* about the scheme; it never decides whom to *let in*. A publicly reachable `WEB_PORT` therefore lets anyone open a cleartext session that skips the terminator's certificate entirely, and no amount of proxy trust reasoning catches that — only the firewall does. Recording it next to `WEB_PORT` is what stops a future contributor from reading the gate as a boundary and opening the port. **Increment 4 is the code change.** Four anonymous surfaces (`base.html` header, `home.html` landing, `welcome.html`, `login.html`) hard-linked to `/signup/`, and there was no way for a template to know the policy because `site` was passed per-view only — the two views that happen to need it, nothing else. The fix is one context processor exposing a single boolean `signup_open`, derived exactly as `signup()` gates it and as nodeinfo already derives `openRegistration`, so the CTA, the form and what we tell the federation cannot drift apart. Two deliberate calls inside it: expose the **bit, not the `SiteSettings` row** (a template holding the settings object gets invited to render whatever the view never approved), and **hide rather than redirect** when closed — dangling a button onto a page that says "not accepting new signups" is the failure mode the owner named, so the closed state simply has no signup affordance, while `Log in` stays exactly where it was on every surface. The first-run wizard needed no special case: a fresh instance defaults to `open`, so the CTA still shows before any admin exists and still routes the operator to `/setup/`. **Verified live on the deployed container, not the tree** — flipped `signup_policy` to `invite`, counted signup links on all four surfaces (2/2/2/1 → 0/0/0/0) with login links unchanged at 2/1/1/1, confirmed `/signup/` still renders its closed notice and a `POST` 403s with no account created, then flipped back to `open` and saw the CTAs return. **A side measurement worth keeping:** the owner asked whether `https://reeltalk.minnix.dev` should already work. It should not, but probing it confirmed the whole chain ahead of the cutover — DNS resolves, TLS terminates on the `*.minnix.dev` LE cert valid to 2026-11-01, `openresty` answers, and the request **lands in our container's logs as `192.168.1.141:… 400 Bad Request`**. That is a second and much more direct confirmation than R74's `/proc/net/tcp` reading that **NPM presents as `192.168.1.141`**, the exact address `TRUSTED_PROXIES=192.168.1.141/32` has to match. The 400 is Django's own `DisallowedHost` under the deployed `ALLOWED_HOSTS=['localhost','127.0.0.1','192.168.1.138']`, which `DOMAIN=reeltalk.minnix.dev` will replace. **`X-Forwarded-Proto` forwarding remains unverified** — the 400 still fires before anything scheme-dependent renders, exactly as in R74; the https actor ID over real HTTPS is the check and it belongs to the cutover. **Gate:** `ruff check` / `format --check` / `makemigrations --check` clean, pytest **805 passed + 5 skipped in 341.67s** — baseline 794 + 11 (10 new cases + 1 clean-room case for the new module, which reports 100% coverage).
- **R77 — Deploy-readiness increment 5: a backup with no restore is a hope, so the restore is built before it is drilled — and it must never be able to guess its target (owner brief + owner decision 2026-09-20).** The brief's headline finding was verified before any code was written: `backup_database` was the repo's only management command and `pg_restore` appeared in **no** non-test code, so increment 5 could not "run a restore and check it" — there was nothing to run. **The decision was raised to the owner before building rather than picked silently**, on two axes: (a) a `restore_database` command vs hand-driven `pg_restore` in the docs vs a command that *cannot* touch the live DB, and (b) a throwaway database in the existing cluster vs a separate throwaway container. Owner chose **the command** and **the in-cluster throwaway**. Rejected on (a): docs-only, because nothing then verifies the procedure is correct, the drill can't be re-run cheaply, and a typo lands on the operator at the worst possible moment; and the cannot-touch-live variant, because it is useless for the actual disaster scenario, which is precisely restoring *into* the app's database. Rejected on (b): a second container, for the extra moving parts and the network hop, when a separately-named database in the same cluster is never the target and is dropped by name. **(1) The asymmetry is the entire design.** Dumping the live database is harmless; restoring over it destroys the owner's imported watchlist. Therefore the target is **never defaulted**: `--into <db>` is required with no default, naming the live database additionally requires `--force`, and the target must already exist — the command never creates one, so a typo in `--into` is a loud connection failure rather than a fresh empty database nobody is looking at. Comparison is exact and case-sensitive because the wire-level `database` startup parameter is resolved that way against `pg_database`, so `--into ReelTalk` cannot silently land on a live `reeltalk`. **(2) pg_restore flags chosen for loudness and atomicity:** `--exit-on-error` (pg_restore's default is to carry on and exit 0), `--single-transaction` (all-or-nothing, so a mid-way failure leaves the target empty rather than half-loaded), `--no-owner` (drops the dump's `ALTER OWNER` so a restore under a role other than the dumper's still works). Connection params come from Django's live settings exactly as in `backup_database`; the password goes through `PGPASSWORD`, never argv — and the command echoes its own argv precisely *because* the secret is not in it. **(3) The drill, run on the real stack without ever targeting live:** fresh dump via the `backup` service (`reeltalk-20260921T134126Z.dump`, 693,255 bytes) → `CREATE DATABASE reeltalk_drill` → restore → **24 public tables identical to live row for row** (1385 films / 1385 shelf films / 9 statuses / 2 users / 2 follows) → app booted against it with `-e POSTGRES_DB=reeltalk_drill` on `:3132`, entrypoint reporting **`No migrations to apply`** (proof the dump carries a current schema, not just data) and serving restored content (`GET /film/7/` → `Take the Trash (2008)`, `GET /` → 200) → teardown (container stopped, `DROP DATABASE`, cluster back to `postgres`/`reeltalk`/`template0`/`template1`, nothing left publishing `:3132`) → **live row counts re-diffed identical to the pre-drill baseline**. All four guards were also exercised live rather than only in tests: `--into reeltalk` refused with a message naming `--force`, `--into ""` refused, missing `--into` refused, and a nonexistent dump path refused before any database was touched. **(4) A gotcha that would bite in an emergency: every service has its own image tag.** The first restore attempt failed with `Unknown command: 'restore_database'` because only `web` had been rebuilt — `backup` is a separate tag (`reeltalk-backup`) built from the same Dockerfile. This is the same no-bind-mount trap that governs every tree edit here, newly relevant because `backup` is the service an operator reaches for at 3 a.m. `DEPLOYING.md` §7 states it. **(5) `DEPLOYING.md` written** — topology, prerequisites (the LE wildcard cert expiring **2026-11-01**, and why Cloudflare must stay grey-cloud or `TRUSTED_PROXIES` has to be rewritten to edge ranges), a full env table, NPM's must-forward list, the port-binding/firewall expectation, first-deploy steps, the build gotcha, backup procedure, the restore procedure with the drill recipe, invite-only setup, a post-deploy verification checklist, and the known limits named rather than hidden. `.env.example` also had a **live footgun**: its backup section told the operator to restore with `pg_restore -d reeltalk <dump file>` — straight into the live database — and now points at the command instead. **(6) A live defect on the invite-only path, found by checking that the documentation was true rather than by looking for bugs.** While writing "invite-only means an admin creates the account" I verified that path on the deployed image and it was broken: `User` was registered with a bare `admin.ModelAdmin` with no password handling, so the add form exposed the model's `password` column as a plain **visible** `AdminTextInputWidget` (not even a masked input), required, with no `clean_password` and no `save_model` override — measured as `COLUMN VALUE = 'hunter2'`, `HASHED = False`, `LOGIN WOULD WORK = False`. Creating a user from `/admin/` therefore wrote plaintext into the column *and* produced an account that could not log in. Owner pulled the fix into this increment. The fix uses Django's own auth forms rather than hand-rolled hashing: `InviteUserCreationForm` hashes through `set_password` and restates signup's identity rules (30-char cap, `LOCALNAME_RE`, case-insensitive duplicate rejection) so an admin cannot mint a name other instances would treat as a distinct identity; `AdminUserChangeForm`'s `password` is a read-only hash field whose `clean_password` returns the stored value, so plaintext cannot be written from the admin at all. Three deliberate exclusions ride along, each for a reason rather than for tidiness: **`private_key` appears on no form** (nothing in the admin needs to read a signing key, and putting one on screen invites a copy into a screenshot or a support ticket); **`localname` is read-only after creation** (it *is* the federated identity — renaming orphans every published URL and invalidates the mirrors other instances hold); **`raw_summary`/`summary` are read-only** because `summary` is rendered from markdown by the profile-edit view, not by `save()`, so editing the raw source in admin would desync them permanently. Re-verified on the deployed container as the exact reversal of the finding: the add form exposes only `display_name`, `email`, `localname`, `password1`, `password2`, both password fields `PasswordInput`, and `'password' in add.fields` is **False**. **Durable lessons:** *(a)* **test the restore, don't document it** — an untested restore path is an assumption about bytes you have never read back, and the drill is what converts it into a fact; the round trip now runs in the suite so it stays proven. *(b)* **A destructive command's default is its most dangerous feature** — `backup_database` can safely read `settings`, so copying that shape into a restore would have made "no target given" mean "destroy the live database". *(c)* **Check that your documentation is true before you write it** — the admin defect existed only in a sentence I was about to assert, and verifying the sentence is what surfaced it. *(d)* **Rebuild every service, not just `web`.** **Tests:** `reeltalk/tests/test_restore.py` — 18 cases: argv shape and connection flags, the live-target guard including exact case-sensitivity and empty-name refusal, password-in-env-not-argv and target-not-live via a stubbed subprocess, refusal *before* anything is invoked, plus a real round trip that dumps the pytest database, creates a scratch database, restores, compares the whole per-table row-count map, and asserts the scratch database does not survive the suite. `reeltalk/tests/test_admin_users.py` — 14 cases driven through the real admin HTTP endpoints: hashed creation, no plaintext left on the row, the created account authenticates, no privileges granted, the D1 shelf bootstrap still fires, unusable and case-insensitively-duplicate localnames rejected, confirmation mismatch rejected, an edit cannot swap the hash for plaintext, an edit cannot rename, `private_key` on no form, and the federation fields not submittable. Three of the restore tests failed the first gate — an off-by-one on `argv[-2:]` vs `argv[-3:]`, `argv[argv.index("-p")]` instead of `+ 1`, and expecting `SystemExit` where Django's `OptionParser` turns the argparse failure into `CommandError`. All three were bugs in my own assertions, not in the command, and were fixed and re-gated rather than the assertions being loosened. **Gate:** `ruff check` / `format --check` / `makemigrations --check` clean, pytest **841 passed + 5 skipped in 344.29s** from a fresh `docker compose run --rm web` on the rebuilt image — baseline 805 **+36** (18 restore + 14 admin + 4 clean-room cases for the two new files). **Still open:** the cutover itself (increment 6), and `X-Forwarded-Proto` forwarding remains unverified exactly as in R74/R76 — the https actor ID over real HTTPS is still the only proof and it belongs to the cutover.
- **R78 — Deploy-readiness increment 6: the cutover — probe the unverifiable link before the destructive step, and never let a destructive operation be the first thing that learns the answer (owner brief + four owner decisions 2026-09-21).** The instance moved from a LAN IP with no TLS to `https://reeltalk.minnix.dev`. **Four decisions were raised to the owner before anything destructive rather than picked silently**, and the fourth of them reshaped the whole sequence. **(1) Fresh database by rename, not drop.** `ALTER DATABASE reeltalk RENAME TO reeltalk_legacy_20260921` then `CREATE DATABASE reeltalk` matching the original exactly (owner `reeltalk`, `UTF8`, `en_US.utf8`). Chosen over DROP/CREATE-with-dump-as-rollback because the old rows then survive *readable and in-cluster*, so rollback is a rename back in seconds instead of depending on a restore path; the drilled restore stays available as a second line. Rejected: a separate compose project and volume — most isolation, most moving parts, and it forces a port/NPM swap at the end. **(2) Short planned outage** — `web` + `worker` stopped only for the swap and migrations; the 1670-row import and the D11 backfill run after the app is back, so they do not extend the window. **(3) Retire instance B**, keeping its volumes. The reason is identity, not tidiness: B holds A mirrored as `http://192.168.1.138:3030/user/minnix/`, and after the cutover A publishes `https://reeltalk.minnix.dev/user/minnix/` — two different actors as far as B is concerned, so the mutual-follow state becomes a stale duplicate rather than a live peer. B was retired **before** the DB swap so it could not deliver stale activity into the fresh database. **(4) Probe the scheme before destroying anything.** R74/R75/R76 had each to leave `X-Forwarded-Proto` forwarding unverified because the `DisallowedHost` 400 fires before anything scheme-dependent renders. The brief's own order verified it *last*, after the database was already gone. Instead the cutover env was applied to the **existing** container with the old data intact — fully reversible by an env flip — and the actor document read over real HTTPS: `https://reeltalk.minnix.dev/user/minnix/`, no `:3030`, on every URL including the keyid, with the negative case taken alongside (same header from the LAN → stays `http`). Had it come back `http`, the destructive work had not started. **Measured rather than assumed, going in:** the cert was re-checked live (`CN=*.minnix.dev`, LE `YE2`, to 2026-11-01) instead of taken on trust; a full text-column scan established what is *actually* IP-keyed — **all 12 occurrences were instance-B mirrors and test debris, and the owner's own account has empty `actor_url`/`inbox_url` derived from `DOMAIN` at runtime**, so R52 holds for the owner's data and fresh + re-import is genuinely clean; and the brief's claim that the container already ran `TRUSTED_PROXIES=192.168.1.141/32` turned out to be wrong — it was `[]` with `SECURE_PROXY_SSL_HEADER=None`, neither key present in `.env`, so the cutover had to **add** the trust list rather than confirm it. **Two operational choices worth keeping.** The first account came from the `/setup/` wizard run by the owner personally, because a fresh database has no superuser and the admin cannot create the first one — a chicken-and-egg the brief's "create my account through the admin" could not satisfy; `signup_policy=invite` was set immediately after migrations because a fresh instance defaults to `open`. The import ran through the **service layer** (`parse_tmdb_csv` + `import_film_csv` under `manage.py shell`, checksum verified both sides) rather than a browser session, because the view is a thin wrapper — same code path, no CSRF dance, no password in a command line. **Result:** 1670 rows → **1378 created / 292 skipped (TV)**, backfill to **1367/1378 posters**, all five §11 checks green, LAN plain HTTP still served without `Secure` on the cookie while real HTTPS carries it, `3030` refused from the public IP, `openRegistration: false`. **The cutover changed no tracked files** — `.env` is gitignored — which is why the only code this session produced is R79. **Durable lessons:** *(a)* **put the unverifiable check before the irreversible action** — the whole reason this cutover was safe is that the one thing nobody could prove in advance was proved against a state that could still be rolled back; *(b)* **verify the brief's measurements too** — two of them were wrong in ways that changed the work; *(c)* **a rollback path you can read is better than one you must execute.** **Rollback position:** `reeltalk_legacy_20260921` (whole pre-cutover DB, directly readable) + `/tmp/env.pre-cutover.bak` (exact previous `.env`).
- **R79 — Three security tests were passing because of an ambient default, and the cutover is what found them (owner decision 2026-09-21).** The first gate run against a production-shaped `.env` returned **3 failed, 838 passed, 5 skipped** on a tree unchanged since R77's green 841 + 5. Not flakiness: turning on `TRUSTED_PROXIES` exposed that three tests never actually pinned the control they were named for. **Two stacked confusions, established by A/B rather than inferred** — the same three tests pass with `TRUSTED_PROXIES` cleared and fail with it set. *(a)* **`RequestFactory` defaults `REMOTE_ADDR` to `127.0.0.1`**, so `pass_through(None, ...)` never produced a missing remote address; it produced loopback, which is inside the `0.0.0.0/0` trust list the test passed, so the middleware correctly trusted it and kept the header. `test_a_missing_remote_addr_is_not_trusted` asserted a property of a case it never constructed. *(b)* **`request.scheme` is a cached property read after the `override_settings` block exits**, so the assertion computed against the **ambient** `SECURE_PROXY_SSL_HEADER` — a value the operator's `.env` sets. While no trust list existed that was `None`, the header was ignored, and the test passed for a reason unrelated to the gate. The two `test_activitypub` RFC 9421 cases were the serious ones: they called `verify_request()` on a hand-built request that **never passes through the middleware**, so under a config where the header is genuinely believed the raw function *does* bless a plain-http delivery — the comments asserted the gate's protection without ever running the gate. **Production was never vulnerable**, verified live in the same session with the middleware actually in the chain (trusted terminator → `https`; same header from the LAN → `http`). The control worked; the tests did not model it — which is precisely why this was fixed rather than explained away. **Owner chose the strictest of three options: make the tests model the real chain.** `pass_through` now deletes `REMOTE_ADDR` for the missing-address case and reads the scheme **inside** the override so the cached value is pinned to the settings under test; `test_activitypub.py` gained a `DEPLOYED` override and a `_through_gate()` helper, and the two RFC 9421 cases now run through `TrustedProxySchemeMiddleware` from an untrusted peer. **Non-vacuity proven, not assumed:** under the deployed posture a *trusted* peer keeps the header and the same delivery verifies `True`, while an *untrusted* peer has it stripped and gets `False` — so the `False` is caused by the gate, not by an inert header. **Hermetic both ways:** 54 passed with the production `.env`, 54 passed with `TRUSTED_PROXIES` cleared. **Gate:** `ruff check` *All checks passed!*, `ruff format --check` *89 files already formatted*, `makemigrations --check` *No changes detected*, pytest **841 passed + 5 skipped in 357.91s**. **Durable lesson: a test that passes because of an ambient default is worse than no test** — it advertises coverage for a control while depending on the absence of the very setting that control exists to govern, and it will fail (or worse, silently keep passing) the moment someone deploys for real. Any assertion that depends on a setting the operator controls must either pin that setting explicitly or exercise the code that reads it. Corollary for this repo: **run the suite against a production-shaped `.env` at least once per deploy-readiness increment**, not just against the dev defaults.
- **R80 — Deploy-readiness increment 7: the whole search surface becomes members-only, superseding the anonymous half of R29 (owner brief + owner decisions 2026-09-21).** `DEPLOYING.md` §12 had named this as a known limit since before the cutover. **This decision supersedes R29's anonymous half**: R29 deliberately gave anonymous users results "without actions" — no click-through link, no watchlist button — and pointed their suggest rows at `/search/?q=<title>` so the dropdown stayed useful to a stranger. That was a reasonable shape for a LAN-only instance. **It is not a reasonable shape for a public one:** every one of those anonymous reads spends the instance's single shared, rate-limited, paid TMDB quota, and with signup closed the stranger has no account to spend it from, so the cost lands entirely on the owner. R29's *authenticated* behaviour is unchanged; only the anonymous half is withdrawn. **The gate is deliberately two different shapes, not one.** `film_search` takes plain `@login_required` — it serves a navigation request, and a 302 to `/login/?next=/search/%3Fq%3Dblade` is the correct and useful answer there. `search_suggest` must **not** take that decorator: it answers an `XMLHttpRequest`/`fetch` whose callback parses JSON, and a redirect would deliver the login page's HTML into a `.json()` call — the client breaks in a way that looks like a server bug rather than an auth refusal. It carries an explicit `if not request.user.is_authenticated: return JsonResponse({"error": "login required"}, status=401)`, placed **ahead of** the `len(query) < 2` check so the auth decision is never masked by input validation — an anonymous `?q=a` gets 401, not the 200-with-empty-array it used to. Placing the guard first also means no code path reaches `search_films()` before the guard runs, which is the property the tests assert with a key configured and `responses` active. **The header box is removed rather than disabled.** Hiding the `<form class="global-search">` for anonymous users deletes the affordance instead of leaving a box that fails when used; `search.js` needed no change because `initSuggest()` already bails when the form is absent, so the client-side suggest path simply never starts. **The layout consequence was the real trap and is worth recording as a pattern:** `.header-top` is `grid-template-columns: 1fr minmax(0, 34rem) 1fr`, so deleting the middle child makes auto-placement drop `.header-user` into the 34rem column, and `justify-self: end` — which was correct for column 3 — now right-aligns inside column 2, putting "Log in" in the middle of the page. The fix pins `.header-user` explicitly: `grid-column: 3` at the base, `grid-column: 2` inside `@media (max-width: 40rem)`, because that breakpoint's grid declares only two columns. **Explicit line numbers rather than `-2`**: a negative line index reads identically at both breakpoints while resolving against a different track count, which is exactly the kind of thing that looks right and silently isn't; and a base `3` left unoverridden on the phone opens an *implicit* third track, leaving a gap on the right that is easy to miss because nothing overflows. Verified by measurement rather than by eye alone — headless Chromium against the real stylesheet, both variants at both widths: the handle's right edge lands on the grid's content edge in all four cases (**1200px** desktop, **374px** at 390px), identical for member and anonymous, so the marquee corner does not shift when the search box appears. **Per R79, the new tests prove non-vacuity rather than just asserting the refusal:** the 401 case includes a member request to the same route returning 200 with rows, so the 401 is shown to be caused by the gate and not by a broken view; the redirect case asserts `len(responses.calls) == 0` so it proves the quota was *not* spent, which the status code alone cannot. Five previously-anonymous tests moved to the `login` fixture so they keep testing what they are named for instead of tripping over the new gate. **Standing constraints held and re-checked on the same deploy, not assumed:** nothing touched `request.scheme` as the app's only scheme source (R75), cookies stay scheme-permissive so plain-HTTP LAN access keeps working (`COOKIES_FOLLOW_SCHEME` deliberately unset), and `http://192.168.1.138:3030/` still returns 200 with the same 401/302 search behaviour as the https origin — the gate is scheme-independent. **Gate:** `ruff check` *All checks passed!*, `ruff format --check` *89 files already formatted*, `makemigrations --check` *No changes detected*, pytest **842 passed + 5 skipped in 365.39s** — baseline 841 + 1. **Verified live over real HTTPS:** `/search/suggest/?q=blade` → `401` `application/json` `{"error": "login required"}`; `/search/?q=blade` → `302 …/login/?next=/search/%3Fq%3Dblade`; anonymous home header → **0** occurrences of `global-search`, brand + "Log in" only. **One loose end flagged rather than fixed:** `templates/welcome.html` step 1 still links to `{% url 'film-search' %}`, so an anonymous visitor to the tour is now redirected to login by a link labelled "search page" — consistent with a closed door, but not with the label. **Raised to the owner and left as-is (2026-09-21).** **Durable lesson: a "known limit" written down before a deploy is a debt with an interest rate** — the same sentence was harmless while the instance was a LAN IP and became a live quota leak the day it was public. When a limit's danger depends on a fact about deployment, the limit should be closed *by* the deploy, not merely re-noticed after it.
- **R81 — The home rail becomes a showcase, not a doorway: strangers see the titles, members follow them (owner request 2026-09-21).** The owner logged out to look at what a stranger sees and liked the rail — trending titles and popular genres are exactly what help someone decide whether a place is worth joining — but did not want those rows to be a path into the catalog. **The change is about the affordance, not the access**; see the boundary note below, which matters more than the change itself. Implementation is one `user.is_authenticated` switch in `templates/home.html`: a member gets `<a class="trending-film" href="/film/<id>/">` / `<a class="genre-pill" href="/genre/<slug>/">`, a stranger gets the same text in a `<span>` with the same class. **A `<span>` rather than a dead or disabled `<a>` on purpose** — a styled anchor with no destination still takes a tab stop, still announces as a link to a screen reader, and still paints the target URL in the status bar on hover, which is precisely the "clickable" signal the owner asked to remove. **Two CSS traps, both live in this file and both easy to miss:** *(a)* the pill was styled by the **element** selector `.genre-pills a`, so changing the tag would have silently dropped the border, the radius, the background and the uppercase tracking and left bare text where a pill used to be — fixed by moving the styling onto a `.genre-pill` class carried by both the anchor and the span, so there is one styling source and the pill looks identical either way. *(b)* **`:hover` matches `<span>` just as happily as `<a>`**, so the existing `.trending-film:hover` (red + underline) and the pill's amber-fill hover would have made the deliberately-dead text light up under the cursor exactly as a link does — arguably worse than before, because now it *looks* interactive while doing nothing. Fixed by scoping both hover rules to the anchor: `a.trending-film:hover`, `a.genre-pill:hover`. **Verified live rather than assumed:** anonymous `GET /` returns **0** occurrences of `href="/film/` and **0** of `href="/genre/`, while the content still renders (`<span class="trending-film">Alien from L.A.`, pills for Adventure / Family / Science Fiction each still carrying their `title="N reviews in …"`); a headless-Chromium hover probe on the live page shows the span's computed `color`, `background` and `text-decoration-line` **identical before and during hover**, with `cursor: auto` rather than `pointer` — the light-up is genuinely gone, not merely untested. **Tests:** the two assertions in `test_anonymous_home_shows_the_rail_and_a_cta` that required those hrefs flipped to forbid them; added `test_anonymous_rail_rows_are_spans_not_anchors` (span present, anchor absent for both row types) and `test_signed_in_rail_links_to_films_and_genres` — the latter is the non-vacuity half per R79, without which the anonymous assertions could be satisfied by a rail that never renders a link for anybody. **Gate:** `ruff check` / `format --check` / `makemigrations --check` clean, pytest **844 passed + 5 skipped in 369.73s** — baseline 842 + 2. **THE BOUNDARY THIS DOES NOT MOVE: `/film/<id>/` and `/genre/<slug>/` are still public.** Removing the link removes the affordance; it does not remove access — anyone handed or guessing a URL still reads the page. If the intent is ever "strangers must not browse the catalog," that is a separate and larger decision, because **`film_detail` cannot simply take `@login_required`**: it serves the **Film wire document to ActivityPub clients by content negotiation (D15/R40)** so a remote instance can resolve a referenced film object by its id URL, and that fetch is anonymous. Gating the HTML means gating only the HTML branch and leaving the AP branch open, or accepting that remote lookups of our films break. **Asked and decided 2026-09-21: leave them public** — consistent with wanting strangers to see what the instance is about, and it keeps federation fully intact. Recorded in `DEPLOYING.md` §12 as a named limit rather than left implicit.
- **R82 — Deploy-readiness increment 8: invite links, who may send them is a setting, and the live policy turned out to have been read wrong (owner brief + owner decisions 2026-09-21).** `SiteSettings`' docstring had said since before the cutover that `invite` meant "closed: accounts are created by an admin — the invite mechanism itself is a later, deliberate step." This is that step. **Owner's four requirements:** a member sends from their own profile; the admin chooses whether all members may or only the site admin; the text reads "Invite a fellow movie freak to ReelTalk" with an **INVITE** button; clicking mints a link with a code the sender pastes into whatever they already use, landing the invitee on the signup page; and one link opens exactly one account. **Chosen shape (owner picked all four):** `/invite/<code>/` as the shareable URL rather than `?invite=` on `/signup/`; **7-day** expiry as well as single-use; **Admins only** as the default scope; and a **read-only** admin ledger with no revoke button, since deleting the row already covers clearing an invite and an unused one expires by itself.

  **The single decision that carries the most weight is the row lock, and it is worth being precise about why it isn't paranoia.** `Invite.lock_live(code)` does `select_for_update` and the signup view holds it inside the same `transaction.atomic()` that creates the account and calls `invite.redeem(user)`. The naive shape — check `is_live`, then create, then mark used — is correct right up until two strangers open the same pasted link in the same second: both read `used_at IS NULL`, both pass, and an invite-only instance has handed out a seat the owner never approved. This is an invite-only instance, so that is not a theoretical race, it is the exact scenario the feature exists to control. Per **R79** the lock is *shown* rather than asserted: `test_lock_live_is_a_real_row_lock_not_a_plain_filter` runs under `transaction=True` and requires `TransactionManagementError` when `lock_live` is called outside an atomic block — if it were a bare `.filter().first()` it would return quietly and the whole guarantee would be a comment instead of a property.

  **`invite_scope` is orthogonal to `signup_policy`, and the two are not the same knob.** `signup_policy` decides whether a link is *needed*; `invite_scope` decides who may *make* one. "Only the site admin" is implemented as `is_staff` — the accounts that can reach `/admin/`, which is this instance's working meaning of admin — and `may_send_invites()` checks `is_authenticated` itself as well as the decorator, so a caller that forgets the decorator still cannot open the door to an anonymous request. Default is deliberately conservative: on a live invite-only instance the owner should decide to widen the circle, not discover it was wide open.

  **A valid link is deliberately independent of `signup_policy`.** Making the link honour the setting would mean that an owner flipping signup open later silently invalidates every link already sent — a way for a settings change to break things the settings change was not about. The link is the stronger statement, so it wins. Verified live under the real closed setting, not only in a test: with `signup_policy=invite`, a freshly minted link still renders the signup form.

  **Two shapes worth keeping in mind for anyone extending this.** *(a)* The mint is `POST`-only because it is not idempotent — every click makes a new code, so a `GET` that mints lets a page render, a crawler, or a browser prefetch spend invites. *(b)* The mint redirects back to the inviter's own profile with `?invite=<code>`, and rendering that code is gated **again** in `_invite_context` on the code belonging to the requesting user. That second gate is not cosmetic: without it the profile page becomes an oracle that renders any code handed to it, and someone could confirm a guessed code is real just by observing whether a link appears.

  **Two environment constraints shaped the copy button, both standing constraints doing the work.** `CSP_SCRIPT_SRC` is `'self'` with no `'unsafe-inline'`, so an inline `onclick` would be silently blocked and the button would appear to do nothing — the JS lives in `reeltalk/social/static/js/invite.js`. And `navigator.clipboard` exists only in a secure context, which the permanently-plain-HTTP LAN origin at `192.168.1.138:3030` is not, so the select-and-`execCommand` fallback is the **required** path on that origin rather than a courtesy for old browsers. The button degrades to leaving the text selected with a "Press Ctrl+C" label rather than failing silently.

  **The finding that outranks the feature.** Verifying the deploy meant *reading* the live policy rather than restating the record, and it came back `signup_policy = open` with `openRegistration = true`: **the public instance was accepting account creation from anyone**, against a cutover record that says `invite` was set immediately after the cutover migrations. **The owner confirmed 2026-09-21 this was deliberate at the time** — they had opened it to see what the page looked like to an anonymous visitor who wanted to sign up — and had it set back to Invite-only. **The durable lesson is not about who flipped it:** a security-relevant value that lives in a database row is invisible to code review, invisible to `git diff`, and invisible to the test suite, because none of those read production. A deploy verification that recites the recorded value instead of querying the live one will happily certify a door that is standing open. Live checks in this file now read the actual value and quote it.

  **Also fixed in the same commit, because it is what prompted the look.** Every `<a class="btn">` rendered as underlined text while its `<button class="btn">` siblings did not: the sheet set the control's colour, fill and bloom but never overrode the browser's underline on anchors, so the same visual control had two looks depending on which tag it happened to use. Fixed on the shared `button, .btn` rule so the tag stops mattering — which also de-underlines the film page's `Edit` and the two `Sign up` CTAs, the consistent outcome rather than a side effect. **Measured, not eyeballed,** with headless Chromium against the real stylesheet at 1280px and 390px: `a.btn` → `text-decoration-line: none`, `button.btn` → `none`, with a plain `<a>` in the same page still computing `underline` as the control that proves the probe can see an underline at all (per R79 — an all-`none` result from a probe that cannot detect underlines would have proved nothing). **The first probe run was wrong and the reason is worth recording:** `page.setContent()` leaves the document at `about:blank`, which refuses to load `file://` subresources, so the stylesheet never applied and every number reported was the browser default wearing our clothes. Fixed by writing the probe page to disk and navigating to it, and by asserting `.invite-box`'s computed background on every run so a silently-missing stylesheet cannot pass again.

  **Gate** (fresh `docker compose run --rm web`, all services rebuilt): `ruff check` *All checks passed!*, `ruff format --check` *90 files already formatted*, `makemigrations --check` *No changes detected*, pytest **882 passed + 5 skipped in 395.72s** — 35 new tests, no pre-existing test changed state.

  **Verified live over real HTTPS on `reeltalk.minnix.dev`:** minted as `minnix`, redeemed by an anonymous client — landing page showed "minnix invited you to join ReelTalk" plus the form, `POST` → `302 /welcome/`, `created_by = minnix | used_by = r82probe | used_at = 2026-09-21 20:47:42Z | live = False`; a second stranger on the same link got no form at all, just *"That invite link has already been used — each one opens exactly one account."* Unknown code → 200 with "not valid" and no form; malformed code → `404` at the route, no lookup. **R80 re-checked on the same deploy rather than assumed:** `/search/` still `302`, `/search/suggest/` still `401 application/json`; plain-HTTP LAN still `200` with the same behaviour, so the invite routes are scheme-independent too. Probe account and invite deleted afterwards (`Invite.objects.count() = 0`, no stray accounts). Two `curl` artifacts recorded so nobody chases them as bugs: the first `POST` 403'd because Django does strict `Referer` checking over HTTPS and `curl` sends none, and the 64-char form token against the 32-char cookie is Django's CSRF masking working as designed. **After the policy was set back to Invite-only:** `openRegistration = False`, `/signup/` renders the closed notice with no form, `POST /signup/` returns 200 with no account created, and all four CTA surfaces carry `signup=0` with `login` intact (`/` 2, `/welcome/` 1, `/login/` 1, `/about/` 1). Cert re-checked rather than trusted: `CN=*.minnix.dev`, `notAfter=Nov 1 02:26:19 2026 GMT`.

  **Docs, and the same interest-rate lesson as R80.** `DEPLOYING.md` §10 had been asserting *"Invite-only in v0.1 means closed: an admin creates the account. There are no invite links or codes,"* and also had `POST /signup/` returning **403** when it returns **200** with the closed page — the gate is the view declining, not the CSRF layer. Both rewritten, with the link flow, the scope table, and the ledger documented. R80's point generalises: a limit or a behaviour written down before a deploy is a debt with an interest rate, and this one was two sentences from being followed as an operating instruction after it stopped being true.
